a:T838,方法很简洁，关键是把回归目标从“mixture -> 最终 loss”改成“当前训练状态 -> 下一阶段 loss”。RegMix 原始做法是：采样若干 mixture，训练若干小 proxy 模型到固定步数，只记录终点验证损失，再拟合一个回归器预测某个 mixture 的终点 loss，最后选一个全程固定的 mixture。RegMix-D 则在 proxy 训练中按多个时间点记录 validation loss，把每条 proxy trajectory 切成若干相邻区间。对于给定的 switch point 数 N，会选出 N+1 个时间节点 t1...tN+1，并从每条轨迹构造 M×N 个训练样本。回归器 f_N 的输入是当前步点 t_l、当前 mixture m、当前 observed loss l_l，输出是下一时间点的 loss l_{l+1}。也就是说，它学的是局部转移，而不是全局终点映射。

在部署上有两个版本。Offline 版本在目标训练开始前递归生成完整 schedule：从初始 loss 出发，在每个 switch point 上搜索使预测下一段 loss 最低的 mixture，把预测出的 loss 再喂给下一段，直到生成整条 mixture schedule。这个版本不需要 target run 中途干预，但误差会递归累积。Online 版本则在 target training 的每个 switch point 上，用目标模型真实观测到的 validation loss 替代预测 loss，再调用同一个冻结回归器选择下一段 mixture。这样做的好处是把 proxy-to-target 的偏差限制在单段决策里，而不是沿整条 schedule 传播。

方法上的几个细节值得注意。第一，作者没有引入额外 RL、bandit 或 bilevel optimization 机制，仍然是回归 + candidate search，所以工程接入成本低。第二，初始阶段固定使用 human-curated Pile prior，而不是从第一个 segment 就让回归器自由选 mixture，这反映了一个现实假设：训练初期没有足够 target-state 信息，先验配比更稳。第三，mixture 搜索仍然通过 Dirichlet-sampled candidates 完成，说明作者追求的是在现有 RegMix 搜索框架内最小改动地升级为 dynamic mixing，而不是重新设计连续优化器。b:T7d0,The key move is to replace a global regression target with a local transition model. In standard RegMix, each sampled mixture is used to train one proxy model to a fixed horizon, only the final validation loss is kept, and a regressor maps mixture to endpoint loss. The chosen mixture is then used for the entire target run. RegMix-D logs validation loss at multiple points along each proxy trajectory. For a chosen number of switch points N, it selects N+1 time anchors and converts each proxy trajectory into M×N training examples. The regressor f_N takes the current state (training step t_l, current mixture m, current loss l_l) and predicts the next loss l_{l+1}. In other words, it learns one-step loss transitions rather than a direct mixture-to-final-loss map.

This enables two deployment modes. In the offline mode, the schedule is generated before target training: starting from an initial loss, the method searches over candidate mixtures at each switch point, picks the one with the lowest predicted next-step loss, feeds that predicted loss into the next decision, and recursively builds a full schedule. In the online mode, the same frozen regressor is queried during target training, but the current loss is the actually observed target-model validation loss rather than the previously predicted one. This grounds each decision in the real target trajectory and limits error accumulation.

Several design choices are worth noting. The method does not introduce RL, bandits, or bilevel optimization; it stays within the regression-plus-search framework, which keeps the engineering overhead low. The first segment uses the human-curated Pile prior instead of a learned mixture, reflecting a cold-start assumption that early in training there is not enough target-state information to trust dynamic adaptation. Candidate mixtures are still searched via Dirichlet sampling, so the contribution is a minimal but effective extension of RegMix rather than a full redesign of the optimizer.c:T571,The experiments follow the standard RegMix setup: a 1B-parameter target model trained on 25B tokens from the Pile. The main comparisons are static RegMix versus RegMix-D, with both offline and online variants evaluated under different switching granularities. The headline findings are consistent. First, RegMix-D outperforms RegMix at every tested granularity, both in validation loss and across 13 downstream tasks. Second, the online variant is generally more reliable than the offline one because it uses observed target losses to correct the state, whereas the offline schedule can accumulate recursive prediction error. Third, the method is notably more proxy-efficient: 128 proxy models, about 25% of the 512-proxy budget used by RegMix, are already enough to surpass the static baseline.

The important experimental point is not one unusually large gain, but the consistency of the improvement. The ablation logic also matters: the benefit comes from extracting more supervision from the same proxy runs, not from adding a new expensive optimization loop. By reusing intermediate losses, each proxy run yields many local transition labels. Another useful observation is that the gains from dynamic mixing are not uniform across training, which supports the paper’s premise that the preferred domain composition shifts over time and cannot be captured well by a single static mixture.d:T4b0,This one deserves a close read. Start with the introduction and Figure 1 to frame the problem correctly: static mixture selection is limited because the optimal domain composition drifts during training. Then focus on the RegMix-D section, especially how trajectory samples are constructed, what f_N predicts, how the offline recursive schedule is generated, and how the online variant replaces predicted losses with observed target losses. The main innovation is not a sophisticated model class; it is the reformulation of mixture search as local state-transition prediction. In the experiments, prioritize three pieces: validation-loss comparisons, the 13-task downstream table, and the proxy-budget ablation. If time is limited, first understand the offline-versus-online trade-off, then look at sensitivity to the number of switch points N. The deeper questions are whether the regressor is truly learning stage-dependent domain demand rather than just smoothing loss trends, whether very fine switching causes schedule instability, whether proxy-to-target transfer still holds at larger scales, and whether the human-prior cold start can be replaced by a more principled initialization strategy.e:T416,The method starts with a bi-level formulation where the outer problem chooses tokens to minimize validation loss and the inner problem trains the model on the selected data. Selection is represented by a relaxed vector alpha under a keep-ratio constraint, while the optimization procedure maps naturally to discrete extreme points. Instead of differentiating through the inner optimum and estimating inverse-Hessian terms, BLADE introduces the inner optimality condition as a constraint and moves it into a penalized single-level objective through Lagrange multipliers. This yields a first-order, Hessian-free update and leads to an excess-loss-like scoring rule based on the discrepancy between a proxy model and a reference model. The important design choice is that the reference model is dynamically synchronized with the proxy and incorporates validation information, rather than being trained once and frozen. For online batch selection, the paper instantiates the method with a memoryless randomized block-coordinate Frank-Wolfe algorithm.f:T46c,The model uses a block diffusion factorization. A sequence is split into contiguous blocks. Previous blocks are kept clean and used as prefix context, while the current block is corrupted with an absorbing [MASK] process and denoised in parallel. The conditional distribution for each block is trained with a weighted cross-entropy over masked positions, with weights induced by the diffusion noise schedule. This design interpolates between AR generation and fully parallel diffusion. The main method is block-size curriculum learning. Training starts with small blocks, where the model sees a generation problem closer to AR and can learn coherent reasoning trajectories. It then gradually increases the block size so the model adapts to coarser parallel denoising. This differs from fixed small-block training, which limits inference parallelism, and from direct large-block training, which is brittle for reasoning. The paper also introduces RelaxedConfidence, an analytical decoding probe that commits contiguous spans of high-confidence tokens earlier under relaxed thresholds when neighboring tokens provide reliable support.10:T472,The analysis starts from a simplified residual recurrence, h_{n+1}=h_n+ε W φ(h_n), where the same matrix W is reused at every step. After unrolling, h_N=h_0+ε∑r_n. The squared norm contains a quadratic term, ε²/d · ||∑r_n||². In an ordinary non-shared residual stack, different residual branches are approximately independent, so this term grows like Θ(ε²N), which motivates ε=1/√N. In a looped network, the same W generates all updates. The activations are produced by the same recurrence, the residual directions remain positively correlated, and the quadratic term grows like Θ(ε²N²). Keeping the residual stream bounded therefore requires εN=O(1), i.e. ε=1/N. The same accumulation appears in the optimization analysis: the output change after one optimizer step scales as ηεN, so ε=1/N makes the stable learning rate roughly independent of N. For a practical block with L unique layers repeated N times, the paper separates two effects: a linear N factor from within-layer reuse and a √L factor from across-layer random-walk accumulation. This yields ε=λ/(N√L) and a learning-rate law η≲1/(λ√L).11:T816,方法上，FSN 保留了 Kuramoto attention 的 score map，只改 value/update law。每个 token 的隐状态不是实向量，而是 k 维相位向量，位于环面上；对应的复数表示是单位模 phasor。注意力分数仍由 query/key 相位相干性、门控和 rotary drift 组成，softmax 保持因果约束。核心变化在更新方向。基础 Kuramoto 层的更新是对 attended token 相位做吸引式耦合，形式上是对 sin(theta_u - theta_t) 的加权和，目标是相位一致。FSN 则引入带谐波的复值耦合核，对每个被关注位置 u，不再主要耦合到 z_u，而是耦合到 z_{u+1}，也就是该位置的后继 token 状态。作者证明，这等价于 Kuramoto–Sakaguchi 耦合里引入一个 frustration angle，而这个角度不是手工参数，而是由数据自身的局部转移 delta_u = theta_{u+1} - theta_u 决定。这样一来，模型不是把当前位置拉向“和检索片段一致”，而是拉向“相对检索片段应当偏移多少”。

更细一点看，FSN 的更新核包含两部分：w0 对当前 attended token 的静态 frustrated coupling，w1 对 successor 的一阶延迟耦合；两者都支持多个谐波 n=1..N，默认 N=3，系数是逐坐标的复数。极坐标下，模长对应 harmonic gain，相位对应 frustration angle，因此每个系数都能直接映射到同步文献中的耦合对象。这个设计的一个优点是可解释性比普通 MLP value mixing 更强。另一个优点是它严格包含若干特例：N=1, w0=1, w1=0 退化回 Kuramoto attention；更一般的实系数对应 Daido harmonic coupling；w0=0 则是纯 successor coupling。作者还讨论了初始化问题：当 frustration angle 全在零附近时，角度方向存在局部对称，早期优化容易不稳定，因此初始化需要打破这种对称性。文中还有一个去掉 FFN 的变体，用对 learned collective modes 的 gated coupling 替代前馈层，想测试性能到底来自 frustrated coupling 还是来自常规 Transformer block 的其余部分。12:T809,FSN keeps the score map from the base Kuramoto attention and replaces the update law. Token states live on a k-dimensional torus as phase vectors, with unit-modulus complex phasors as the working representation. Attention scores are still computed from phase coherence, learned gates, and rotary phase drift, under a causal softmax. The main change is in how attended information acts on the current token.

In the base Kuramoto layer, the update is an attractive coupling term, a weighted sum of sin(theta_u - theta_t), which pushes phases toward agreement with attended tokens. FSN replaces this with a harmonic complex-valued coupling kernel and a one-step delay. For an attended position u, the model can couple not only to z_u but also to z_{u+1}, the successor state. The key theoretical point is that coupling to the successor is equivalent to Kuramoto-Sakaguchi coupling with a frustration angle given by the data transition delta_u = theta_{u+1} - theta_u. So the offset is not a free learned bias; it is induced by the sequence itself. This turns the layer from consensus-seeking to transition-seeking.

The kernel has two parts: a static frustrated coupling term over the attended token itself, and a delayed coupling term over successors. Both are expanded over harmonics n=1..N, with per-coordinate complex coefficients; the default uses N=3. In polar form, magnitudes are harmonic gains and phases are frustration angles, which makes the layer unusually interpretable relative to standard value mixing. The construction strictly contains several special cases: standard Kuramoto attention when N=1, w0=1, w1=0; Daido-style harmonic coupling for real coefficients; and pure successor coupling when w0=0. The paper also discusses an initialization issue: zero frustration angles create local symmetries in the early loss surface, so initialization must break that symmetry. A variant without the FFN replaces the feed-forward block with gated coupling to learned collective modes, to test how much of the gain comes from the coupling law itself.13:T6ca,The experiments are character-level language modeling on text and code, with matched-parameter and matched-epoch comparisons. The headline result is on enwik8 at roughly 1M parameters: FSN beats a tuned Transformer at matched epochs, 1.6050 vs 1.6258 bits per character, and also after convergence, 1.5953 vs 1.611. The most informative analysis is the copy-depth decomposition, where positions are bucketed by the longest suffix that appeared earlier in context. This is exactly the regime where attention is expected to help. Kuramoto attention loses badly to the Transformer in high copy-depth bins, by as much as +0.4231 bpc. FSN reverses that pattern and outperforms the Transformer in every bin with depth four or greater. That strongly supports the paper’s central claim: successor-based frustrated coupling fixes a predictive deficiency rather than merely improving average optimization.

The authors also run a parameter-doubling ladder up to 8M parameters, where the matched-epoch advantage persists. Training cost is the main downside. FSN is several times slower per epoch. However, when measured by wall-clock time to reach the Transformer’s mean loss, FSN needs only 0.70x the time at 4M and 8M, while requiring 1.23x and 1.56x at 1M and 2M. So the layer is more expensive per step, but some of that is recovered through faster convergence at larger scales. The no-FFN variant reaches 1.6452 bpc on enwik8 versus 1.6258 for the Transformer baseline, suggesting the frustrated coupling itself carries substantial modeling power, though the full FSN remains clearly better. The paper is explicit about its limits: no subword tokenization, no large-scale models, and no evidence yet under modern large-LM training settings.14:T859,框架名为 Diffusion-Proof，由两个 7B 模型组成：dLLM-Prover-7B 负责整段证明生成，dLLM-Corrector-7B 负责局部修补。底座是 Fast-dLLM-V2-7B。整体思路不是用一个 diffusion 模型包打天下，而是把“写初稿”和“修局部错误”拆成两个子任务，这个拆分很合理，因为 formal proof 中很多失败并不是全局思路错，而是局部 tactic、变量、类型或中间步骤不一致。

训练数据方面，作者从既有 theorem proving 数据中收集了 5.5M records，再清洗筛选出 300k SFT 数据用于 whole-proof fine-tuning。这里能看出作者没有直接把全部数据灌进去，而是做了质量筛选；对 formal domain 来说，这通常比盲目扩量更重要，因为错误证明、低质量中间轨迹和格式噪声会直接污染 verifier-sensitive 学习。

方法创新主要在 large block correction。作者不是做 token-level 或很小span的 infilling，而是训练 dLLM-Corrector 在较大 diffusion block 上进行局部修正。这样做的理由有两个。第一，形式化证明中的错误常常跨越多个 token，甚至一整段 tactic 组合，小span修补不够。第二，diffusion 模型天然能利用左右双向上下文，因此适合在“前后都已知，中间有错”的条件下重写局部片段。与 AR 模型相比，这种修补不需要从错误点之后整段续写，理论上更稳，也更符合 proof debugging 的工作流。

推理流程是：先由 dLLM-Prover-7B 根据 theorem statement 生成完整证明；如果 verifier 判错，但判断为 proof skeleton 大体正确，则调用 dLLM-Corrector 对局部块进行修复。这里的关键不是单次生成，而是“生成 + 验证 + 局部修补”的闭环。这个闭环和代码生成里的 generate-then-repair 很像，但在 formal proof 中更自然，因为 verifier 给出的成功/失败信号更干净。

从建模范式上看，这篇文章真正想说明的是：diffusion LM 的优势不只是并行生成，而是双向条件建模和 block-level rewriting。formal proving 只是一个高约束测试场景。15:T7b9,The framework, Diffusion-Proof, uses two 7B diffusion models built from Fast-dLLM-V2-7B: dLLM-Prover-7B for full-proof generation and dLLM-Corrector-7B for local proof repair. This split is sensible. In formal proving, many failures are not due to a completely wrong global plan; they often come from local inconsistencies, invalid tactics, typing issues, or broken intermediate steps. A dedicated correction model matches that error structure better than a single monolithic generator.

On the data side, the authors collect 5.5M theorem-proving records from prior work and clean/select 300k examples for SFT. That choice matters. In verifier-sensitive domains, low-quality trajectories and formatting noise can be more harmful than raw scale, so the paper implicitly treats data curation as part of the recipe.

The main technical idea is large-block correction. Instead of token-level or very small-span infilling, the correction model is trained to rewrite larger proof blocks using both left and right context. This is where diffusion modeling has a natural advantage over AR generation: it can condition bidirectionally and rewrite a local region without forcing a full left-to-right continuation from the first error onward. For formal proofs, where an error often spans a whole tactic chunk rather than a single token, large-block repair is a more realistic operation.

Inference follows a generate-then-verify-then-repair loop. The prover first generates a complete proof from the theorem statement. If the proof fails verification but appears structurally close, the corrector is invoked to repair local blocks. The important point is not just using diffusion for generation, but using it as a bidirectional rewriting engine under verifier feedback.

At the paradigm level, the paper is really arguing that the practical advantage of diffusion LMs is not merely parallel generation. It is block-level conditional rewriting with access to both past and future context.16:T4d0,The headline results are reported against AR baselines trained on the same data. Under pass@32, Diffusion-Proof improves by 1.61% on ProofNet-Test and 6.14% on MiniF2F-Test. These are not huge numbers, but they are meaningful in formal proving, where success rates are low and exact verification makes gains hard to obtain. The larger gain on MiniF2F suggests that local repair and coherence may matter more on harder or more brittle benchmarks.

The paper also reports solving one IMO problem that DeepSeek-Prover-V2-7B could not solve. This should not be overinterpreted as a broad ranking claim, but it does suggest that the diffusion-based error profile may differ in useful ways from AR provers.

From the visible setup, the model scale is two 7B models, with 5.5M raw records and 300k selected SFT examples. The most important ablations to inspect in the full paper are: prover-only versus prover-plus-corrector, large-block correction versus smaller-span repair, and the cost/benefit trade-off of verifier-guided correction. If the paper includes analyses by proof length, error type, or correction success rate, those are more informative than pass@k alone because they reveal what kind of failures diffusion actually fixes.17:T53a,The paper makes three concrete corrections. First, it improves prompt quality. The authors identify ambiguous prompts in the original benchmark and rewrite them to be more distinctive, while also introducing a templated prompting scheme that better matches model-specific prompting practices. The point is not to make the task easier in an artificial way, but to reduce variance caused by poor prompting so the score better reflects model capability.

Second, it changes metric aggregation. The original Physics-IQ score is defined at the dataset level, which means samples can contribute unevenly and failure modes are hard to localize. The paper introduces a sample-level Physics-IQ Verified score so that each sample and each metric can be analyzed more directly and aggregated more fairly. This improves interpretability and statistical hygiene.

Third, it cleans artifacts in the ground-truth videos. The authors show that many reference videos contain spurious activations unrelated to the target physical phenomenon, such as incidental motion or visual noise that can trigger IoU- or MSE-based metrics. They therefore clean the references so the metrics respond more selectively to the intended physical effect. This is a pragmatic benchmark-repair strategy: clean the measurement target before redesigning the entire metric stack.18:T50f,The most important result is not that one model scores higher than another, but that model rankings change materially once the benchmark is corrected. The authors evaluate six image-to-video models under both the original and the verified protocols and find that models respond differently to the benchmark fixes, leading to substantial ranking shifts. That means the noise in the original benchmark is not harmless random noise; it can systematically favor some models over others.

The scale of the benchmark issues is also nontrivial. The paper reports that the refinement removes measurement errors in 57.6% of all samples, affects 29.8% of videos, and corrects more than 34.8% of prompts judged to be highly ambiguous. The benchmark itself contains 66 physical experiments, each recorded from 3 viewpoints and repeated twice, for a total of 396 videos. Each 8-second video is split into a 3-second conditioning segment and a 5-second continuation to be predicted. The evaluation still uses the original four metrics—Spatial IoU, Spatiotemporal IoU, Weighted Spatial IoU, and MSE—but revisits how they should be aggregated and what reference signal they should operate on. The practical takeaway is that these are not cosmetic fixes; they are large enough to alter benchmark conclusions.19:T79f,There are three core technical ideas. First, the task is cast as a POMDP. The model runs an Observation-Thought-Action loop, where actions request raw media slices such as frames, audio segments, or short clips. Crucially, the environment only serves raw media extraction; perception, reasoning, and action selection all happen inside the same model. The most important design choice is the strict split between transient multimodal percepts and persistent textual memory. High-dimensional observations are consumed locally within a turn, distilled into text, and then discarded. Long-horizon state is therefore carried by the reasoning trace rather than by retained raw pixels or audio tokens.

Second, the model is bootstrapped with Agentic SFT built from best-of-N trajectory synthesis plus dual-stage quality control. This is more than a data-generation detail. In active perception, answer correctness alone is not enough: a trajectory may reach the right answer through poor observation choices, and a plausible trajectory may still produce weak memory compression. The dual filtering is intended to keep trajectories that are both correct and learnable, so the model acquires action syntax, observation timing, and memory-writing behavior before RL refinement.

Third, the paper introduces Agentic RL with TAURA. The authors argue that vanilla GRPO is poorly matched to multi-turn agentic reasoning because it assigns a uniform trajectory-level advantage, which blurs the distinction between pivotal discovery turns and trivial actions. They call this advantage homogenization. TAURA uses turn-level entropy as a steering signal to rescale advantages, giving more weight to uncertain and potentially decisive turns. Relative to standard GRPO, this is a more targeted credit-assignment mechanism for interleaved perception and reasoning. Relative to pure supervised trajectory imitation, it preserves room for policy improvement and exploration.1a:T78d,The experimental story has four notable parts. First, the model improves over the direct Qwen2.5-Omni baseline on all ten reported benchmarks. Representative numbers in the paper include 50.5 on LVBench and 71.1 on MLVU. On omni-modal understanding, it reports gains of +4.7 on DailyOmni and +7.8 on OmniVideo. On LongVALE temporal grounding, the gain is an absolute +33.4. This suggests the benefit is not confined to one narrow task type; it extends across long-video QA, audio-visual understanding, and temporal localization.

Second, the gains are tied to efficiency rather than brute-force scale. A 7B OmniAgent reportedly outperforms Qwen2.5-VL-72B on LVBench, 50.5 versus 47.3, while using 73% fewer frames. That is the most practically meaningful result in the paper. It indicates that a learned active-perception policy can trade far fewer raw visual tokens for better task performance, which is more interesting for foundation-model design than another attention-side optimization alone.

Third, the framework shows positive test-time scaling. The paper reports a +6.2 improvement on VideoMME-Long when allowing more inference-time interaction steps. This is important because it extends the idea of spending extra compute at test time from text-only chain-of-thought into multimodal active perception.

Fourth, RL and TAURA appear to matter beyond cosmetic gains. From the full paper narrative, SFT alone teaches basic action execution, but policy quality degrades in longer multi-turn settings through redundant browsing and weak credit on key discovery turns. RL improves the policy, and TAURA further helps by distinguishing pivotal turns from routine ones better than direct GRPO. The excerpt here does not include the full ablation tables, but the most important comparisons to inspect are passive input versus active perception, SFT-only versus SFT+RL, GRPO versus TAURA, and alternative memory/action-space designs.1b:T437,The framework has three stages. First, a Hybrid Disentangled VQ-VAE learns discrete action codes from adjacent egocentric video frames. A frozen DINOv2 encoder extracts spatial features, and a spatiotemporal Transformer models short-term frame transitions. The encoder uses two sets of learnable queries, one for action and one for background. These are quantized by two separate VQ bottlenecks, producing an action codebook and a background codebook. A mask-guided decoder encourages the action branch to focus on physically relevant motion while pushing camera motion and background changes into the background branch. Second, the learned action codes supervise VLM pretraining: the model maps observations and language instructions to high-level action intent. Third, during robot adaptation, the pretrained intent representation is combined with DINOv2 visual feedback and a flow-matching action head to produce continuous robot controls. The intent-perception decoupling is important: the human-derived code is used as a semantic action prior, not as a direct robot command.1c:T45b,The paper first runs controlled interventions on ThinkMorph. Removing visual thoughts or replacing them with Gaussian noise barely changes accuracy across nine benchmarks, and in some cases text-only reasoning is slightly better. Attention analysis shows that subsequent text tokens attend heavily to the visual-thought region regardless of its semantic content. The authors then compare completion distributions under two contexts: a teacher context that includes a privileged visual-thought trace, and a student context that only includes the original image and question. A non-trivial KL gap shows that the generation pathway contains distillable distributional information. Visual-OPSD uses this gap through on-policy self-distillation. Teacher and student share the same weights but use different conditioning contexts. The student samples trajectories under its own text-only policy, and token-level JSD transfers the VT-conditioned teacher distribution onto those on-policy tokens. At inference time, the model runs in text-only mode with no diffusion steps, no architectural change, and no extra parameters.1d:T644,The core method is Act2Answer. Existing VLM knowledge benchmarks are converted into short tabletop episodes where the agent answers by taking a simple placement action among candidates instead of generating text. This design serves two purposes. First, many VLAs do not expose a clean text-generation interface, so text QA is not a faithful evaluation path. Second, by reducing the task to a short action episode, the benchmark minimizes confounds from long-horizon planning, motor execution, and environment complexity.

The benchmark contains 1,720 unique binary questions across 12 categories, including attribute, state, color, symmetry, shape, emotion, celebrity, living world, counting, time, traffic, and public information. The emphasis is diagnostic control rather than embodied realism.

The analysis has two layers. At the behavioral level, the paper compares 7 modern VLA systems against 9 strong VLM baselines to measure retention gaps across knowledge categories. At the representation level, it introduces layerwise intent probing: linear probes are trained on per-layer representations to predict the correct answer for each episode. This helps distinguish between two failure modes: the information is gone, or the information is still present internally but is not translated into the correct action.

A useful conceptual framing in the paper is the decomposition of embodied task success into perception, knowledge, control, and environment. Act2Answer is explicitly designed to hold control and environment complexity down so that knowledge-sensitive behavior becomes more measurable.1e:T68c,There are three main empirical takeaways.

First, current VLAs tend to remain reasonably strong on simple perceptual categories, but they show noticeably larger degradation on richer semantic categories relative to their source VLMs. In other words, low-level visual distinctions such as color or shape are retained better than broader commonsense or world-knowledge judgments. From the provided full text, the reliable quantitative facts are the evaluation scale: 7 VLAs, 9 VLM baselines, 1,720 binary questions, and 12 categories. The paper explicitly claims substantially larger VLA-VLM gaps on richer semantic categories, but the exact per-category numbers are not visible in the excerpt and should not be invented.

Second, VQA co-training is associated with stronger performance on knowledge-sensitive embodied tasks. This is an important recipe-level observation: action fine-tuning alone does not guarantee preservation of the knowledge interface, while continued QA-style supervision appears to help maintain it.

Third, the layerwise probing results suggest that answer-relevant information can remain linearly decodable from internal representations even when the final action is wrong. That points to a readout or interface problem, not only catastrophic forgetting. For foundation-model researchers, this is the most interesting result in the paper because it separates representational retention from behavioral accessibility.

When reading the full experiments, the key things to verify are whether the VQA co-training effect is consistent across model families, whether the VLA-VLM gap is uniform across categories, and where in depth the probe accuracy peaks.1f:T60b,Built on a pretrained text-to-audio diffusion transformer trained with flow matching over VAE latent tokens. The target audio is encoded to z0 and noised to zt; K reference clips are encoded with the same VAE to rk and kept clean. The transformer input is a single concatenated token sequence [zt; r1; ...; rK] with full self-attention across target and references. References are distinguished by lightweight slot identity embeddings (one learned vector per reference slot, added to all tokens of that reference) rather than speaker encoders/adapters. A single holistic natural-language prompt describes the whole scene and refers to voices as “reference 1/2/...”.

Core technical contribution: identifying the “reference shortcut” in reference-conditioned flow matching. Under the commonly used logit-normal timestep distribution, training mass concentrates on low-to-moderate noise where zt still preserves speaker cues; the model can pick the correct reference by acoustic similarity, achieving low loss while ignoring text-based role binding. Since generation starts from pure noise, this shortcut is unavailable at test time. The authors localize the shortcut with a probe trained on frozen audio features, showing robust reference–target matching across the low/moderate noise range, and then run noise-schedule ablations showing binding metrics improve monotonically as training shifts to higher noise. They fix it by using a high-noise-biased timestep distribution (a Beta+Uniform mixture) to force reliance on text for binding.20:T44d,The public write-up does not disclose parameter counts, data size, token count, RL algorithm details, or reward-model architecture. The key reported number is that the beneficial-trait RL model improves over the compute-matched baseline on 44 out of 53 internal and external benchmarks. These benchmarks cover deception, honesty, reward hacking, latent safety risks, harmful agentic behavior, specification compliance, health, mental health, and related safety-relevant behaviors. On held-out in-distribution beneficial-trait evaluations, the model improves on traits such as truthfulness, corrigibility, and metacognitive transparency. The more informative result is transfer to independently constructed evaluations with different domains, tasks, and grading setups. Reported health evaluations include realistic medical conversations, physician-written rubrics, and high-confidence medical errors. Mental-health evaluations measure both disallowed content and beneficial support. The model is also reported to be harder to steer toward harmful behavior through adversarial prompting or fine-tuning.21:T402,The paper starts from a binary-reward analysis. For GRPO with group size g and per-rollout success probability p, the expected within-group advantage variance is p(1-p)(g-1)/g. When p is close to 0 or 1, groups are likely to have identical rewards and contribute little group-relative signal. The empirical design isolates SFT depth as the main variable. The authors train ladders of SFT checkpoints with the same architecture, data, hyperparameters, and evaluation protocol, then run the same GRPO recipe from each checkpoint. The main ladder uses Qwen2.5-Coder-3B-Base; a contrastive ladder uses DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Base. On Qwen, deeper SFT increases pre-RL pass@1 but reduces the best post-GRPO pass@10, producing a rank inversion. On DeepSeek, the checkpoints remain in a safer regime and the GRPO rankings compress rather than invert. KL regularization and label smoothing are tested as interventions, but they do not rescue the collapsed Qwen checkpoints, indicating that the failure is upstream of these GRPO variants.22:T5b3,The Qwen2.5-Coder-3B-Base study uses five SFT depths and three seeds. The DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Base study uses four matched depths and three seeds. SFT uses 5,000 medium-difficulty KodCode-V1 problems, decontaminated against HumanEval and MBPP with an embedding cosine threshold of 0.70, dropping 20.8% of the pool. Chain-of-thought traces are regenerated with Gemini 2.5 Flash, with a median length of 518 tokens, to fit a 2,048-token context. Training uses BF16 LoRA with rank 128 and alpha 128 on all linear layers and embeddings, AdamW 8-bit, learning rate 1e-5, batch size 16, a constant schedule, weight decay 0.001, and a 100-step format warmup. The GRPO data is drawn from held-out KodCode-V1 problems and decontaminated in three passes. A middle checkpoint performs 16 stochastic rollouts per problem at T=1.0 and top_p=0.95; only problems with pass_count from 1 to 14 are kept, giving 1,096 Qwen records and 1,104 DeepSeek records. Each checkpoint is trained for 400 GRPO/DAPO steps with group size 8, beta 0, learning rate 1e-6, and gradient clipping at 0.1. On Qwen, peak GRPO pass@10 falls from 0.806 to 0.481 as SFT depth increases, even though pre-RL pass@1 rises. The highest-pass@1 SFT checkpoint loses to shallower checkpoints in every seed. Pre-RL entropy is positively associated with the GRPO outcome. DeepSeek does not show the same inversion, supporting the paper’s claim that entropy collapse, not SFT depth alone, is the risk signal.23:T58d,The paper derives a first-order analysis of token-level entropy dynamics under GRPO. The entropy change of a token decomposes into the product of the trajectory-level advantage and a local entropy sensitivity function determined by the next-token distribution. This yields an advantage-surprisal quadrant view: positive-advantage low-surprisal tokens tend to decrease entropy, positive-advantage high-surprisal tokens tend to increase it, and the signs are reversed under negative advantage. Since low-surprisal tokens occur much more often, the realized update is biased toward entropy reduction. The authors also identify a near-criticality property: a mild perturbation to the weights of specific tokens can flip the direction of entropy evolution; beyond the critical threshold, the weight mostly controls magnitude rather than sign. STARE uses this observation in three steps. It selects entropy-critical tokens using batch-internal surprisal quantiles, amplifies the effective advantage of positive-advantage high-surprisal tokens, attenuates that of negative-advantage high-surprisal tokens, and activates the intervention only when batch-averaged entropy falls below a target. Compared with DAPO-style clip-higher, it remains active even when importance ratios stay close to one in near on-policy training. Compared with entropy bonuses, it does not indiscriminately push up entropy at all high-entropy positions.24:T428,Even though the setting is continuous-domain generative modeling rather than text LLM pretraining, the methodological relevance is high. First, the paper cleanly separates fitting a local regression target under training-time marginals from controlling global properties under the model’s self-generated distribution. That tension also appears in diffusion LMs, non-autoregressive language modeling, and even standard AR pretraining. Second, it reframes RL from being only a preference-alignment tool to being a mechanism for correcting structural blind spots of the pretraining objective. That is directly relevant to the pretrain–post-train loop. Third, the proposed reward is not based on human preference labels but on discrepancies between data and model distributions in a learned representation space. This is a useful template for data-aware RL pretraining, self-corrective continual training, and representation-space reward construction. The contribution is best read as a mechanism paper that clarifies the role of RL relative to the base objective.25:T84c,The work has two layers. The first is theoretical. In a probability-flow ODE setting, the paper studies a property gap of the form |E_pbase[r] - E_q[r]|, where r is a property of interest. The conclusion is that flow/score matching gives weak control over this gap. In the worst case, there is no guarantee at all, because the training loss is measured under interpolation marginals q_t, whereas generation is governed by the model’s own rollout distribution p_t. Once those differ, small regression error under q_t does not imply that sampled outputs preserve the desired property. Under additional regularity assumptions, one can derive a bound, but it degrades with the L2 Lipschitz constant of the reward. Many semantic or perceptual properties are precisely the kind of high-curvature, non-smooth functions for which such guarantees become weak.

The second layer is the method: Discriminator-Guided RL (DRL). Instead of using preference data, DRL trains a discriminator to estimate the density ratio between the data distribution and the current model distribution in a self-supervised representation space. Real samples and model samples are embedded by a pretrained SSL encoder; a discriminator is trained in that feature space to distinguish data from model outputs; the discriminator logit is then used as the reward for KL-regularized RL. The central design choice is to define the reward in SSL feature space rather than pixel space. This makes density-ratio estimation more tractable and constrains the correction to semantically meaningful directions instead of low-level noise.

This differs from preference-based RL in a precise way. PRL optimizes an external reward that often mixes subjective preference with objective quality. DRL instead optimizes what the model distribution is still missing relative to the data distribution, making it closer to a corrective term for the pretraining objective. It also differs from directly modifying the matching loss: RL is used because it evaluates and optimizes the reward on the model’s own sampled trajectories, thereby bypassing the q_t versus p_t mismatch.26:T79a,The empirical study spans several modern image-generation architectures, including SiT, JiT, REPA, and RAE. One notable result is that the discriminator used by DRL can be very simple—even a linear classifier is sufficient to produce consistent gains. That suggests the improvement is not coming from an unusually powerful reward model, but from the way the reward is constructed. Evaluation is based mainly on Fréchet distances in multiple feature spaces, with additional checks using held-out human-preference rewards. The main finding is that DRL improves distribution-quality metrics over the base model across architectures, and these gains transfer to unseen human-preference rewards despite never training on preference labels.

A particularly important experiment compares two pipelines: applying preference-based RL directly to the base model versus applying PRL on top of a DRL-corrected model. The latter yields a better reward–distortion Pareto frontier. The interpretation is straightforward: DRL first recovers structural properties that should have come from the data but were not fully captured by the matching objective, leaving PRL to focus on genuinely subjective preferences rather than spending preference data to repair basic realism or coherence. That has clear implications for post-training pipeline design.

From a foundation-model perspective, the key empirical question is whether the paper really supports the claim that the issue is objective mismatch rather than lack of data. If the full paper shows DRL remaining useful even as the base model gets stronger, that would strengthen the argument further. The excerpt provided here does not include the full numeric tables, so exact gains should not be fabricated. Still, the experimental story is already clear: consistent gains across architectures, simple discriminators suffice, held-out preference rewards improve, and downstream PRL becomes more efficient.27:T433,A good skim starts with the introduction, focusing on the paper’s central claim: RL is often repairing properties that matching-based training failed to capture from the data itself. Then move to the theory section on the property gap. The most important points are the mismatch between training under q_t and sampling under p_t, and why the dependence on the reward’s Lipschitz constant makes many perceptual properties hard to control through FSM losses alone. Once that is clear, the DRL method becomes much easier to interpret. In the method section, focus on three questions: why use an SSL representation space, how the density-ratio discriminator is trained, and how KL-regularized RL is integrated into the existing flow-model pipeline. In the experiments, prioritize the main base-model-versus-DRL results and the DRL+PRL versus direct PRL Pareto comparison. A solid skim should take around 30 minutes. If you work on diffusion LMs, flow-matching language models, or the role of RL in correcting pretraining objectives, the theory section is worth a closer read.28:T78b,ViGOS explicitly decomposes the student output into two stages: a visual description first, then reasoning plus final answer. The supervision is decomposed accordingly. The visual-description segment is supervised by an image-only perception teacher, which has access to the image but not the reference answer. This forces token-level targets in the early segment to be grounded in visual evidence. The later reasoning and answer segments are supervised by a privileged reasoning teacher that can see the reference answer, but only after the student has already produced the visual description. This preserves answer-guided dense supervision where it is useful, while preventing privileged information from directly shaping the early perceptual claims.

The method also uses a reference teacher for invalid rollouts. The rationale is practical: once the student trajectory has drifted into an invalid format or broken task constraints, continuing segmented on-policy supervision may amplify noise. The reference teacher is then used to restore output structure and reduce format drift. This is a stability-oriented compromise between strict on-policy learning and controllable supervision.

A notable aspect is that the paper does more than prompt the model to “describe before reasoning.” It changes the supervision graph of OPSD so that different token spans are scored by teachers with different information access. The paper also introduces PALR, the Privileged Answer Leakage Rate, to quantify how much of the teacher correction on a fixed student rollout is attributable to the privileged answer rather than the image. The point of PALR is not a single headline number, but a way to make shortcut risk analyzable. Relative to vanilla OPSD, the main innovation is teacher-context assignment rather than a new loss. Relative to sparse-reward RL, ViGOS keeps the sample-efficiency advantage of dense token-level supervision.29:T5a3,The evaluation is broad and aligned with the paper’s claim: preserve the gains of OPSD on standard benchmarks while improving robustness to language-prior shortcuts. The benchmarks cover general vision-language ability, expert-level multimodal reasoning, visual mathematics, spatial grounding, and stress tests designed to expose reliance on textual priors. The central empirical result is that ViGOS largely retains the benchmark improvements of vanilla OPSD on standard tasks, while performing better on prior-sensitive evaluations where genuine image dependence matters.

The most informative ablations are the obvious ones. First, a direct comparison against vanilla OPSD tests whether the shortcut problem is real in practice. Second, removing the image-only perception teacher checks whether privileged supervision alone causes the model to regress toward weaker grounding. Third, removing the explicit visual-description interface probes whether the benefit comes from staged supervision or merely from output templating. The provided excerpt does not include full tables, parameter counts, or exact gains, so no precise numbers should be invented here. Still, the experimental pattern is clear: the method is not mainly about pushing one benchmark higher, but about changing the robustness profile—small or no loss on standard sets, better behavior on prior-sensitive ones—which is exactly what the method is designed to achieve.2a:T48d,The method builds on MathNeurosurgery. For each parameter, the authors compute a task-specific importance score using the parameter magnitude together with an activation normalization term derived from input feature norms. They compute these scores separately on math data and non-math control data. Within each layer, they select the top-k important parameters and define math-specific parameters as those important for math but not for unrelated tasks. They repeat this extraction independently for English, German, French, and Hindi, then compare the resulting parameter sets with Jaccard similarity. Three aspects matter here. First, the comparison is done at the parameter-set level rather than representation similarity, so it is closer to asking whether the same circuit is reused. Second, the analysis is both model-wide and layer-wise, which reveals where sharing happens. Third, they include weight intervention experiments to validate that the localized parameters are functionally tied to math reasoning rather than being a statistical artifact. Relative to prior multilingual math work, the novelty is the parameter-level localization across languages.2b:T433,The study evaluates Llama 3.2 1B Instruct, Qwen3 4B IT-2507, and Llama 3.1 8B Instruct, with the main text centered on the 1B Llama model and larger-model results in the appendix. GSM8K is used for math reasoning, across English, German, French, and Hindi; MMLU and RACE serve as non-math controls. The main empirical findings are consistent across settings. English always yields the largest set of math-associated parameters, German and French are in the middle, and Hindi has the smallest set, matching the performance ranking. Pairwise Jaccard overlap peaks in intermediate layers and drops in early and late layers. The overlap stays well above a random baseline for most layers, indicating a genuinely shared substrate for math reasoning. In larger models, the late-layer drop becomes more pronounced, which the authors interpret as stronger language-specific specialization enabled by larger parameter budgets. The paper is more about stable structural trends than a single headline metric, so the key evidence is in the layerwise plots rather than one summary number.2c:T40d,The setup is a one-layer tied-weight autoencoder. Inputs x∈R^n are p-sparse: each coordinate is activated by an independent Bernoulli(p) variable and the active value is drawn from a symmetric mean-zero distribution μ. The encoder is Wx, and the decoder is ϕ(W^T y), with ϕ(t)=t^m for an odd integer m. The population reconstruction loss is rewritten as a constant pnμ2 minus an optimizable term \tilde L(W). The paper first establishes two baselines. If A=W^T W is diagonal, corresponding to no superposition, at most d coordinates can be represented and the optimum is Θ(pd). If the activation is linear, the rank constraint again limits the gain to O(pd). The main theorem then shows that nonlinear superposed representations can achieve much larger scaling: for n>d^m, the optimum is lower bounded by C1 min(p d^m, d p^{1/m}) and upper bounded by C2 p d^m. Under a strong log-concavity assumption on the input amplitudes, there is also an O(d) upper bound, yielding bounds of the form min(p d^m, d p^{1/m}) up to min(p d^m,d).2d:T47f,The paper models Top-k SMoE as a piecewise map defined by affine gating scores. Within a region with a fixed active expert set, the map is continuous; discontinuities occur at score-tie switching boundaries. Discontinuities are classified by order: a pairwise tie between one active and one inactive expert is order-1, while simultaneous ties among more experts define higher-order events. The geometric analysis uses measure-theoretic slicing to estimate the volume of epsilon-thickened discontinuity sets. The key result is that order-1 neighborhoods dominate in the small-epsilon regime, while higher-order neighborhoods vanish in relative measure. The result is extended to l-infinity thickening, which supports efficient logit-based tests for near-tie regions. The stochastic analysis treats perturbations as a diffusion process and shows that trajectories almost surely hit a discontinuity in finite time, with the first hit almost surely being order-1. 标签；因为同一条 proxy run 的中间 loss 被重复利用，单位 proxy compute 产出的监督信号更多。另一个重要观察是，动态 mixture 的收益在训练不同阶段并不均匀，这间接支持了作者的出发点：域最优配比确实会随训练进展变化，静态 mixture 只能做折中。","reading_guide":"这篇建议精读。先看 Introduction 和 Figure reasoning,","starred":false,"priority":"p2","reading_tier":"must_read","source":"arxiv","sources":["arxiv"],"published_at":"2026-06-17T16:34:02Z","category_id":"architecture","cover_url":"/covers/2606_19257.png","hf_upvotes":0,"arxiv_categories":["cs.CL"],"summary":{"core_problem":"这篇论文解决的是 block diffusion language model 在长 CoT 推理中能否使用大 block 的问题。block diffusion 希望把跨 block 依赖做成自回归，把 block 内 token 用扩散去噪并行生成，以换取推理并行度。但大 block 会削弱逐步生成的细粒度约束，直接训练大 block 时推理能力明显下降。论文把 block size 从一个固定超参提升为训练和推理共同作用的尺度变量，研究训练 block size、推理 block size、解码策略三者如何影响长推理。","importance":"这篇工作对预训练研究的价值在于，它给 diffusion LM 做长推理提供了一个较清晰的训练动态结论：并行生成的粒度不能只在推理阶段调大，训练阶段必须让模型逐步适应从细粒度到粗粒度的去噪条件。它不是单纯提出一个 diffusion 解码技巧，而是在 8B 规模上验证了 block size curriculum 可以缓解大 block 训练的脆弱性。对 AR 之外的语言建模范式来说，这是一个重要信号：推理能力不只来自模型规模和数据，也来自生成因子分解与训练课程之间的匹配。论文还指出，当前 block diffusion 的效率收益受 token-level confidence threshold 限制，说明推理并行度的瓶颈已经部分转移到 commit 策略，而不只是模型结构。","technical_approach":"模型采用 block diffusion factorization：序列被切成连续 block，block 之间按自回归顺序建模，前面 block 保持 clean context；当前 block 从全 mask 状态开始，通过吸收态离散扩散过程迭代预测被 mask 的 token。训练目标可化为对 masked positions 的加权交叉熵，权重由噪声日程决定。这个设计保留了 AR 的长程顺序依赖，又允许 block 内 token 并行去噪。核心创新是 block-size curriculum learning。直接用大 block 训练时，模型需要在一个更宽的局部双向空间里同时恢复很多 token，局部约束变弱，长 CoT 中容易破坏中间推理步骤。课程学习先使用小 block，让模型在接近 AR 的细粒度条件下学会推理轨迹，再逐步过渡到大 block，使模型适应更粗粒度的并行去噪。与固定小 block 相比，它保留了大 block 推理的效率空间；与直接大 block 训练相比，它降低了训练不稳定和推理退化。论文还提出 RelaxedConfidence 作为分析性解码 probe：常规解码只在 token 置信度足够高时 commit，策略偏保守；RelaxedConfidence 检查高置信 token 的连续 span，允许被可靠邻域包围的 token 在较宽松阈值下更早 commit，用来估计 block diffusion 还能释放多少并行度。","experiments":"论文训练并开源 DreamReasoner-8B，一个 8B 参数的 block diffusion reasoning model。主要实验围绕数学和代码推理 benchmark，比较不同训练 block size、推理 block size 和解码策略。关键发现是：直接使用大 block 训练会导致推理性能显著下降；小 block 训练能保持较好的长 CoT 推理；从小 block 到大 block 的 curriculum 可以恢复大 block 训练下丢失的推理能力，并让大 block 推理获得效率收益。论文称 DreamReasoner-8B 在数学和代码推理上达到与 Qwen3-8B-Thinking 接近的表现，并明显超过同规模既有 block diffusion 模型。效率分析中，RelaxedConfidence 在 thinking phase 和 answering phase 分别带来平均 TPF，即 tokens per forward pass，22.5% 和 54.5% 的提升，而且没有破坏推理正确性。这个结果说明，模型本身已经能在局部区域给出更宽的可靠并行生成信号，但默认 confidence threshold 没有充分利用。","reading_guide":"建议精读三处。第一是 block diffusion preliminaries 和训练目标部分，重点看它如何把 clean prefix、masked current block、absorbing corruption 和 weighted cross-entropy 组合起来；这决定了它与 full-sequence diffusion LM、纯 AR LM 的差异。第二是 block size 相关实验，尤其是训练 block size 与 inference block size 的交叉表或曲线；这里能看到大 block 失败不是单一解码问题，而是训练条件和推理条件不匹配。第三是 block-size curriculum 和 RelaxedConfidence。前者是主要训练贡献，值得关注 curriculum 的阶段设计、block size 迁移节奏、是否需要额外 compute；后者是推理瓶颈分析，建议看 TPF、正确率和不同推理阶段的拆分结果。需要留意的是，RelaxedConfidence 更像诊断工具，不一定等价于最终可部署解码算法；它的意义在于暴露保守 commit 阈值带来的并行度损失。","authors_note":""},"summary_en":{"core_problem":"The paper studies whether block diffusion language models can use large blocks for long chain-of-thought reasoning without losing reasoning quality. Block diffusion keeps autoregressive dependencies across blocks and denoises tokens inside each block in parallel. This gives a path toward faster decoding, but large blocks weaken the fine-grained sequential constraints that long reasoning often relies on. The central question is how training block size, inference block size, and decoding policy interact at an 8B reasoning-model scale.","importance":"The main contribution is a training-dynamics result for diffusion-based language modeling: coarse parallel generation cannot be treated as an inference-only knob. A model trained directly with large blocks can lose reasoning ability, while a curriculum that moves from fine-grained to coarse-grained blocks can make large-block inference usable. This matters for pretraining research because it identifies block size as a scaling axis for non-AR language models, not merely a decoding hyperparameter. The paper also shows that the efficiency bottleneck is partly in token commitment rules. Conservative confidence thresholds leave parallelism unused even when the model assigns reliable local predictions.","technical_approach":"$f","experiments":"The authors train DreamReasoner-8B, an open 8B-parameter block diffusion reasoning model. The experiments vary training block size, inference block size, and decoding strategy on math and code reasoning benchmarks. The key result is that naive large-block training substantially hurts reasoning, while small-block training preserves reasoning ability. A fine-to-coarse block-size curriculum recovers strong reasoning under larger blocks and enables efficiency gains during inference. The paper reports that DreamReasoner-8B is competitive with Qwen3-8B-Thinking on math and code reasoning and clearly improves over prior block diffusion models at comparable scale. RelaxedConfidence gives average tokens-per-forward-pass gains of 22.5% in the thinking phase and 54.5% in the answering phase without degrading reasoning fidelity, indicating that conservative token-level commitment is a real bottleneck.","reading_guide":"Read the block diffusion formulation first, especially how clean prefixes, masked target blocks, absorbing corruption, and weighted cross-entropy are combined. Then focus on the experiments that cross training block size with inference block size; these are the most important evidence that large-block failure is a training mismatch, not just a decoding issue. The curriculum section is the main technical contribution and is worth checking for schedule design, compute cost, and sensitivity to block-size transitions. The RelaxedConfidence analysis is useful for understanding the remaining inference bottleneck. It should be read as a diagnostic for unused parallelism rather than as a fully settled serving algorithm.","authors_note":""},"recommendation":{"zh":"这篇会直接影响你对 diffusion LM 做 reasoning 的 prior：先别盯采样步数，先复现 block-size curriculum；若结论稳，训练粒度就是比解码并行度更关键的控制杆。","en":"This should update your prior on diffusion reasoning: before obsessing over sampling steps, reproduce the block-size curriculum result—if it holds, training granularity is a more important lever than decoding parallelism."},"metadata":{"institutions":[],"authors":["Zirui Wu","Lin Zheng","Jiacheng Ye","Shansan Gong","Xueliang Zhao","Yansong Feng","Wei Bi","Lingpeng Kong"],"notable_authors":["Lingpeng Kong"],"tags":["diffusion-lm","reasoning","block-wise-denoising","curriculum-learning","long-cot"],"links":{"arxiv":"https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.19257","pdf":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.19257","github":null,"project":null,"source_url":null,"article":null}}},{"id":"2606.18524","title":"On the Residual Scaling of Looped Transformers: Stability and Transferability","abstract":"Looped (weight-tied) Transformers apply a shared residual block $N$ times ($h \\leftarrow h + \\varepsilon\\,f(h)$, same $f$ at each step), increasing effective depth without adding parameters. Prior depth-scaling analyses prescribe $\\varepsilon = 1/\\!\\sqrt{L}$ for depth-$L$ residual networks. We show that this is insufficient for looped architectures: weight sharing makes residual updates correlated across iterations, requiring the stronger scaling $\\varepsilon = 1/N$. 后，稳定学习率不再随 N 缩小。对 L 个 unique layers、每层 loop N 次的实际 Transformer block，论文把两类增长拆开：loop 内共享权重导致线性 N 累积，层间独立性导致 √L 随机游走，于是得到 ε=λ/(N√L)，并推出稳定学习率上界主要随 unique depth L 变化，η≲1/(λ√L)，不随 N scale。","authors_note":""},"summary_en":{"core_problem":"The paper studies residual scaling for looped, weight-tied Transformers. A shared residual block is applied N times, but the usual deep-residual rule ε=1/√N does not keep the residual stream stable and does not make the learning rate transferable across loop counts. The failure comes from weight sharing: residual updates across loop steps are correlated, so they add constructively rather than as an independent-layer random walk.","importance":"The result is relevant to pretraining recipes for recurrent-depth Transformers, Universal Transformer variants, ALBERT-style tying, and latent-computation models. These models try to buy more effective depth without adding parameters, but increasing the loop count often causes activation growth and learning-rate fragility. The paper gives a practical rule: use ε=1/N for a single shared block, and ε=λ/(N√L) when L unique layers are each looped N times. It also clarifies a broader point: depth-scaling laws derived under independent weights cannot be directly reused for parameter-shared networks.","technical_approach":"$10","experiments":"The experiments train decoder-only Transformers on FineWeb-Edu. They test three main predictions. First, activation-norm measurements show that ε=1/√L controls norms in non-shared deep networks, but ε=1/√N fails in looped networks as N increases; ε=1/N keeps the residual stream bounded. Second, pairwise cosine similarities between loop-step updates are densely positive, and this correlation persists through training rather than appearing only at initialization. Third, learning-rate sweeps over N∈{1,2,4,8} show that linear residual scaling keeps the near-optimal learning rate almost invariant, while square-root scaling shifts the optimum and makes training more sensitive. For multi-layer blocks, experiments over L∈{12,24,48} show that ε=λ/(N√L) allows a single learning rate to work well across multiple (N,L) settings. The provided text includes the key loop counts, depths, datasets, and scaling laws, but not the full table of loss values.","reading_guide":"Read Sections 3, 4, and 5 carefully. targets a specific gap in synchronization-based attention: if self-attention can be viewed as a consensus-forming dynamical system, why does Kuramoto-style attention still underperform a standard Transformer precisely on the repetitive regimes where attention should matter most? The authors argue that consensus is good for retrieval but misaligned with next-token prediction. Pulling a token toward the retrieved context tells the model where a similar pattern occurred, but not what came next. FSN changes the target of synchronization from the attended token itself to the successor of the attended token, so retrieval is turned into a transition-aware predictive signal.","importance":"Its value for pretraining research is more conceptual than immediately recipe-level. First, it turns the often-discussed phenomena of representation clustering, rank loss, and layerwise collapse into an actionable modeling hypothesis: attention behaves like synchronization, and prediction may require frustrated rather than attractive coupling. Second, it proposes a different value-pathway semantics for language modeling. Instead of absorbing retrieved content, the model absorbs the offset implied by what followed that content. That is directly relevant to autoregressive modeling, where transitions matter more than static matches. The contribution is still early-stage. instead of generating the whole object once in a fixed autoregressive order.","importance":"This is not a direct pretraining method paper, but it is relevant to modeling paradigms around any-order generation, masked conditional inference, and iterative refinement. It treats next-token conditionals from an LLM as Gibbs transition operators and highlights a limitation of fixed-order autoregressive sampling: it can introduce order bias, recency effects, and distorted dependencies among variables. The framing is useful for understanding where AR language models are a poor fit for structured joint distributions, and it sits close to ideas from masked LMs and diffusion-style iterative generation.","technical_approach":"Large Language Gibbs represents a structured object as variables X=(X1,...,Xn). The method first initializes a state, often using autoregressive generation. It then repeatedly selects one variable Xi, serializes the remaining variables X-i into a prompt, and resamples Xi from the LLM’s conditional distribution. Even when the local conditionals qi(Xi|X-i) are not mutually compatible with any explicit joint distribution, the induced Markov chain can still have a unique stationary distribution under support assumptions. That stationary distribution reflects a compromise among the local conditional beliefs rather than the bias of a single generation order. The paper also discusses prompt designs for variable metadata, type constraints, enumeration constraints, integer ranges, and variants such as block Gibbs or alternative variable-selection schedules.","experiments":"The paper demonstrates the method in three settings. On synthetic distributions, sequential prompting can produce biased marginals and violations of conditional independence, while Gibbs updates substantially reduce these artifacts. For consistent reasoning on benchmarks such as GSM8K and TruthfulQA, related answers are treated as variables and iteratively resampled to encourage global consistency, giving a more principled alternative to recent consistency-oriented algorithms. For Bayesian structure learning from tabular data, the method uses LLM-Gibbs to generate synthetic data and define a world-knowledge-driven prior over causal structures, improving structure learning on real datasets. The provided content does not include the full numerical tables, so the exact improvement magnitudes should be checked in the experiments.","reading_guide":"Skim in about 30 to 45 minutes. Section 5 can be read selectively: use the GSM8K/TruthfulQA part for reasoning consistency, and the Bayesian structure learning part for LLMs as structured priors. memory。需要注意的是，这类方法是否能替代大规模 LLM 的标准 attention，关键取决于困惑度、长度外推、训练稳定性和硬件实现效率；仅有线性复杂度形式还不够。","technical_approach":"Gaussian Mixture Attention 在一个投影后的 routing space 中学习 K 个 Gaussian mixture components。每个 query 和 key 不再两两做点积，而是分别映射为对 K 个组件的 posterior responsibility 向量，形成 ΓQ 和 ΓK 两个 N×K 的非负矩阵。key responsibilities 先把 value 写入 K 槽 latent memory，即计算 (ΓK)^T V，并配合 component-wise normalizer 做归一化；query responsibilities 再从这个 memory 中读取输出。代数上，隐含的 token affinity 是 ΓQ(ΓK)^T，仍可看作 N×N 关系，但实现时不物化该矩阵，而是利用矩阵乘法结合律先聚合到 K 个 latent slots。和 Linformer 这类低秩投影不同，GMA 的低维结构来自概率 mixture routing；和 Performer 这类 kernel 方法不同，它的中间变量是可分析的 responsibility；和 Mamba/S4 不同，它仍保留 attention-like 的内容寻址形式。","experiments":"正文片段显示论文包含对 learned responsibilities 的分析：mixture components 大多被使用，没有明显塌缩到少数组件；hard assignment 与表层 token 类别有中等程度对应，但不能解释为干净的语义类别。论文摘要片段没有提供完整 benchmark、模型规模、训练 token 数、速度/显存数字或语言建模 perplexity 改善幅度。因此目前能确认的是方法设计与责任分配分析，不能据此判断其在大规模 LLM 预训练中的实际竞争力。","reading_guide":"建议泛读，预计 20 分钟。优先看 GMA 的 forward computation、normalization 方式、复杂度分析，以及 responsibility collapse 的诊断图。若目标是长上下文 LLM，要重点检查 causal masking 是否自然支持、KV-cache 或增量解码怎么做、K 随模型宽度和序列长度如何扩展、是否有稳定的大规模语言建模实验。若这些部分证据不足，可把它看作一个有结构的 linear attention 变体，而不是成熟替代方案。","authors_note":""},"summary_en":{"core_problem":"The paper addresses the quadratic token-to-token interaction cost of standard attention. The goal is to avoid materializing an N-by-N affinity matrix while retaining an attention-like and interpretable routing mechanism, instead of moving fully to implicit recurrent or state-space dynamics.","importance":"This is an architecture paper relevant to long-context pretraining. Its useful contribution is to recast token mixing as routing through a small set of latent Gaussian-mixture components. With a fixed number of components K, the dominant sequence-length cost becomes linear rather than quadratic. The method is not just sparse attention or a random-feature approximation to softmax; it builds a K-slot latent memory through probabilistic responsibilities. For large-scale LLM use, the decisive evidence would be perplexity, scaling behavior, length generalization, training stability, causal decoding support, and kernel efficiency. The complexity form alone is not enough.","technical_approach":"Gaussian Mixture Attention learns K Gaussian mixture components in a projected routing space. Queries and keys are mapped to posterior responsibility vectors over these components, producing two non-negative N-by-K matrices, ΓQ and ΓK. Key responsibilities write values into a latent memory by computing (ΓK)^T V, together with component-wise normalization. Query responsibilities then read from the normalized memory to produce token outputs. Algebraically, the induced affinity is ΓQ(ΓK)^T, but the method never materializes this N-by-N matrix; it aggregates through the K latent slots using associativity of matrix multiplication. Compared with Linformer-like low-rank projection, the bottleneck is a probabilistic mixture-routing structure. Compared with Performer-like kernel attention, the intermediate responsibilities are directly inspectable. Compared with S4 or Mamba-style models, it preserves a content-addressed attention-like computation.","experiments":"The provided text indicates that the paper analyzes learned responsibility matrices. The components are reportedly used broadly rather than collapsing to a small subset, and hard assignments show moderate alignment with surface-form token categories, though the components should not be read as clean semantic classes. The excerpt does not provide full benchmark results, model sizes, training-token counts, throughput or memory measurements, or language-modeling perplexity numbers. Thus the confirmed contribution is the mechanism and routing analysis; the evidence is insufficient to judge competitiveness for large-scale LLM pretraining.","reading_guide":"Skim in about 20 minutes. The main lesson is that parallel prediction is not enough. A useful draft tree must preserve causal consistency along each branch. Otherwise, the model may produce tokens that are individually plausible but mutually inconsistent, wasting verification capacity and limiting speedup.","technical_approach":"JetFlow introduces causal parallel tree drafting. Prior head-based speculative decoding methods face a tradeoff. Autoregressive drafters such as EAGLE generate path-conditioned candidates, which improves acceptance, but drafting cost grows with tree depth. Bidirectional block-diffusion drafters such as DFlash generate many positions in one pass, but their branch-agnostic marginals can form inconsistent candidate trees. JetFlow trains a causal prediction head that predicts multiple tree nodes in a single forward pass while using block-level causal attention over hidden states to preserve branch-wise conditioning. This makes each branch closer to the target model’s autoregressive factorization. On MT-Bench, it still reaches τ=5.9 and more than 4x speedup. Existing schedulers often predict output length or remaining decode length and approximate SJF or SRPT, which is effective for mean latency when size estimates are accurate. In reasoning-heavy workloads, decode length is highly variable, workload distributions shift, and bursty arrivals are common. Under these conditions, prediction-driven scheduling can be fragile and may still provide poor control over P90 to P99 TTLT.","importance":"The contribution is mainly on the serving side, but it matters for model research because reasoning-oriented posttraining changes the length and memory profile of inference. Longer adaptive generation increases KV-cache pressure and makes average-centric metrics less informative. The work argues for deployment-aware evaluation of reasoning models: output-length behavior, tail latency, and cache dynamics should be treated as part of the practical cost of a model, not just as an implementation detail.","technical_approach":"The proposed scheduler replaces explicit decode-length prediction with distribution-aware soft priority boosting. It starts from FCFS and assigns each request a smooth boost computed from lightweight observable signals, such as decoded-token progress and historical latency statistics. Requests are then ordered by arrival time minus boost, rather than by predicted final length or remaining length. This avoids hard size-based ranking and is intended to be more robust under partial observability and distribution shift. The system also co-designs scheduling with cache-aware preemption and eviction, since LLM decoding is stateful and memory-coupled. A priority decision that ignores KV-cache capacity can reduce queueing delay but increase TTLT through recomputation and cache thrashing.","experiments":"The visible content reports experiments across heterogeneous workloads with long-tailed decode lengths, bursty arrivals, degraded predictors, and distribution shifts. on-policy 更新、长回答分布、系统利用率耦合。","authors_note":""},"summary_en":{"core_problem":"The paper targets rollout latency in on-policy RL for LLM post-training. Autoregressive sampling is sequential, and a small number of long generations often determines the wall-clock time of a rollout batch. Standard speculative decoding does not transfer cleanly to this setting: the target policy keeps changing, so a fixed drafter becomes stale, and early rollout phases with large active batches can be compute-bound, where parallel verification may be slower than plain AR decoding.","importance":"Its main value for foundation-model research is on the RL post-training systems side. The paper makes a useful distinction between fixed-model serving and RL rollout generation: policies evolve, response distributions sharpen, active batch sizes shrink over time, and tail decoding dominates latency. These details matter for how many rollouts can be collected per unit time, how long responses can be afforded, and how RL recipes scale in practice.","technical_approach":"EfficientRollout uses self-speculative decoding with a drafter induced from the current target policy rather than a separately trained auxiliary model or a history-based cache. The drafter is obtained through weight quantization of the target model, so it stays coupled to the evolving policy while being cheaper to run. The framework adds two control policies. A system-aware toggle enables speculative decoding only in favorable regimes, avoiding slowdowns in large-batch compute-bound phases. An adaptive draft-length policy changes the number of proposed tokens according to the observed acceptance behavior during RL. The paper analyzes block efficiency, the average number of target-distributed tokens produced per draft-verify iteration, together with system timing. This is the main difference from speculative decoding work that primarily optimizes acceptance rate in a static serving setting.","experiments":"The reported gains are up to 19.6% lower rollout latency and up to 12.7% lower end-to-end training latency over an accelerated AR rollout baseline built on common RL training and serving stacks. The target-induced quantized drafter maintains useful block efficiency under policy updates, while adaptive draft length reduces wasted verification when acceptance behavior changes.","reading_guide":"Skim in 25–35 minutes. The core problem is that most memory benchmarks assume a single-user private setting and mainly reward recall and utility, while real deployments often involve multiple principals writing into and querying a shared memory pool. In that regime, a system must not only remember useful facts, but also enforce contextual access boundaries and honor deletion requests.","importance":"Its direct relevance to foundation-model pretraining is limited. The main contribution is on the evaluation side. What it adds is a clearer notion that memory quality is not equivalent to recall quality. For agent systems, memory must be governed. The pretraining-facing takeaway is indirect: if long-term memory, external memory, or episodic memory become more tightly integrated into training, the supervision signal may need to encode authorization and forgetting behavior, not just retention and retrieval.","technical_approach":"The paper introduces a benchmark rather than a new agent architecture. GATEMEM defines shared-memory evaluation along three coupled dimensions: Utility, Access Control, and Active Forgetting. It spans four domains—medical, office, education, and household—and contains 91 long-form multi-party episodes with 2,218 hidden checkpoints. During evaluation, the model receives the interaction history, the current requester identity, and the relevant policy context, but not the query label, grading rubric, or protected target. The probes go beyond direct retrieval and include indirect inference, delegated requests, and social-engineering-style recovery attempts. The forgetting notion is explicitly interface-level: after a deletion request, the agent should no longer recover, confirm, or reconstruct the deleted information behaviorally, without claiming certified physical erasure or full machine unlearning.","experiments":"The main empirical finding is a persistent trade-off among utility, access control, and active forgetting. Across baselines and backbone models, no method performs strongly on all three dimensions at once. Long-context prompting often gives the best overall governance trade-off, but at high token cost. Retrieval-based and external-memory approaches can reduce cost, yet they still show frequent failures in unauthorized disclosure and post-deletion recovery. The paper’s emphasis is less on a single headline score and more on exposing systematic failure modes in shared institutional settings.","reading_guide":"This is a skim paper. In the experiments, it is enough to retain the main pattern: long-context prompting is the strongest but expensive baseline, while retrieval and external memory are cheaper but less reliable on governance. 中一部分文本描述存在歧义，而且没有充分考虑不同模型的提示习惯。作者为不清晰样本重写更具区分性的描述，并引入 template 化 prompting，使输入更符合模型类型与使用规范。这样做的目的不是“帮模型作弊”，而是减少由于提示不当导致的无关误差，让分数更接近模型能力上限。\n\n第二是 metric aggregation 修正。原始 Physics-IQ 的噪声不是均匀噪声，而是会系统性偏向某些模型。\n\n数据层面的修正幅度也不小。文中给出：57.6% 的样本存在被修正的 measurement errors，影响到 29.8% 的视频；34.8% 的 prompt 被认定为高度歧义并得到纠正。基准本身包含 66 个物理实验、3 个视角、2 次重复，共 396 个视频，每个视频 8 秒，其中前 3 秒作为条件输入，后 5 rendering-robust（四种渲染取最差）下掉到 23.5%。论文将差距归因于三类能力缺口：缺少显式的“严格/默认”知识结构（grounding）、难以提出针对性的可废止新假设（novelty）、以及缺少形式化的最小变更信念修正机制（belief It turns open-ended “explain the anomaly” generation into an auditable task with polynomial-time checks (derivation, conservativity, minimality), reducing judge noise and explicitly penalizing theory-destroying explanations. Human labels provide reliable positives for only a small subset of outputs, while the unlabeled pool contains both good and bad responses. At the same time, the judge may prefer superficial features such as verbosity or style. Treating unlabeled samples as negatives, or trusting the judge directly, can therefore reinforce evaluation bias.","importance":"This is not a pretraining method paper, but it is relevant to evaluation pipelines, reward data filtering, and model selection. Its main contribution is to cast a common production setting—few human-verified positives, many unlabeled generations, and a biased automatic judge—as a positive-unlabeled auditing problem. The contribution is methodological and diagnostic rather than a new training recipe.","technical_approach":"PUAudit works on pairwise model outputs for the same prompt. Each pair has an LLM-judge preference, and a small subset also has a human preference. The method focuses on strict A/B preferences and excludes ties because it relies on an ordered winner-minus-loser representation. In a fixed embedding space, it aligns the distribution of human-verified positives with a reliable subset of the unlabeled pool using Partial Optimal Transport. Unlike standard optimal transport, Partial OT does not force all probability mass to be matched. This matters because the unlabeled pool is contaminated by low-quality responses. The method identifies likely latent positives without retraining the judge or modifying the underlying models.","experiments":"The paper reports experiments across multiple benchmarks and bias settings, including judge biases induced by superficial perturbations such as verbosity or style. The main comparison is between raw LLM-judge decisions and PUAudit-corrected decisions in terms of agreement with human preferences. The provided full-text excerpt does not include complete numerical tables, model identities, data scale, or exact improvement margins, so the safest reading is qualitative: the method improves human consistency under biased judging. The important ablations are expected to involve the transported mass fraction, embedding choice, number of human positives, and robustness under stronger bias perturbations.","reading_guide":"A skim read is enough, around 20–30 minutes. In the experiments, check how the bias scenarios are constructed and how much human supervision is required.
